## Page 1


Prepared for submission to JHEP
Excitability in quantum field theory
Jacqueline Caminiti,a Federico Capeccia,b and Jonathan Sorcec
aPerimeter Institute for Theoretical Physics
bUniversity of California, Davis
cPrinceton Gravity Initiative
Abstract:
In quantum field theory, it is not always possible to excite one state out
of another using only local operators. This paper establishes abstract algebraic criteria
for (local) excitability in general quantum theories, and computes these criteria explicitly
for zero-mean Gaussian states in (generalized) free field theories.
We find that in this
context, due to the special nature of Gaussian states, one-way excitability always implies
two-way excitability, and our results generalize the “quasiequivalence theorems” of Powers,
Størmer, van Daele, Araki, and Yamagami.
A key role in our proof is played by the
information-theoretic tool of canonical purification. In appendices, we provide a pedagogical
introduction to the algebraic formulation of (generalized) free field theory.
arXiv:2604.19861v1  [hep-th]  21 Apr 2026

## Page 4


1
Introduction
When can one state of a quantum field be excited out of another? In the algebraic descrip-
tion of quantum field theory, this question can be made precise. One starts with an abstract
algebra A0 describing the field content of the theory, then constructs different Hilbert space
“sectors” by selecting algebraic states and performing the GNS construction. Each GNS
sector Hω can be thought of as the set of local excitations around a single chosen state ω,
so the question of whether ω2 can be excited from ω1 is a question of whether ω2 “lives
inside” the space Hω1.
Concretely, every GNS sector carries a representation of the field algebra A0, and we
say that ω2 can be excited out of ω1 if there is a density matrix ρω2 on Hω1 that reproduces
the ω2 correlation functions:
trHω1(ρω2a) = ω2(a),
a ∈A0.
(1.1)
Beyond asking when excitability is possible, one can also study when the correlation func-
tions of ω2 can be reproduced by a pure state,
ρω2 = |ω2⟩⟨ω2|,
(1.2)
and one can further ask, “if ω2 can be excited out of ω1, does that necessarily mean that
ω1 can be excited out of ω2?”
These are physically important questions at the foundation of quantum field theory,
and the answers are generally nontrivial. One expects, for example, that infrared effects will
prevent the excitation of a thermal state out of the global vacuum in Minkowski spacetime
— see figure 1 — while no such obstruction should occur within a finite causal diamond.
More generally, one expects that any two states that are “well behaved in the ultraviolet”
should be excitable into one other within compact subregions. While these statements are
physically intuitive, it turns out to be quite difficult to establish them with any degree of
rigor, since the abstract question of excitability is hard to translate into standard tools of
quantum field theory like scattering amplitudes and correlation functions.
In the context of (generalized) free field theory, the work of [1–4] proved a theorem
determining when two zero-mean Gaussian states ω1 and ω2 can be mutually excited out of
one another. In honor of the paper [4] that established the result in its most general form,
this is typically called the “Araki-Yamagami theorem.” In Klein-Gordon theory, Verch [5]
used this theorem to establish that any two “Hadamard” states — i.e., Klein-Gordon states
that are well behaved in the ultraviolet — can be mutually excited out of one another
within compact regions.
Interestingly, if one reads the papers [1–5], one will not see a transparent connection
between the questions posed there and the “excitability” questions we have introduced
above. Instead, the Araki-Yamagami theorem concerns the problem of quasiequivalence
of sectors. In free field theory, once one chooses a GNS representation Hω, the abstract
algebra A0 of free fields can be completed to a von Neumann algebra Aω. Within this
algebra, the original abstract fields appear as the unitary “Weyl operators” eiϕ[f], where f
– 2 –

Figure 1: While a thermal state cannot be excited (indicated by ̸≺) from the Minkowski

absence of infrared effects.
is a real smearing function. Two abstract states ω1 and ω2 are said to be quasiequivalent if
the von Neumann algebras Aω1 and Aω2 are related by an algebraic isomorphism that does
not modify any of the Weyl operators. As we will explain in section 2, quasiequivalence
implies mutual excitability, but the argument for this implication is not direct, and it is not
apparent from the analysis of [1–4] whether mutual excitability implies quasiequivalence.
The point of this paper is to revisit the quasiequivalence work in [1–4] using a different
perspective and an updated set of tools. Our motivation for doing so comes from problems
like the geometric modular flow conjecture [6–8], and the holographic construction of large-
N sectors with inequivalent bulk duals [9–11]. These are interesting contemporary problems
that would profit from a better understanding of the conditions under which one state can
be excited into another. The main new contribution of our paper is a series of intermediate
results that establish criteria for excitability even when full quasiequivalence is not satisfied,
and a demonstration that excitability always implies quasiequivalence for Gaussian states in
free theories. Additionally, because our approach is motivated by a different set of questions
and makes use of information-theoretic tools, we also believe that our work provides a
substantial conceptual simplification of the proofs developed in [1–4].
To understand most of this paper, it is necessary that the reader have a background in
algebraic free field theory. To make the paper approachable to readers without this back-
ground, we have provided a complete pedagogical introduction to this theory in appendix
A. The reader may approach section 2 without consulting this appendix, but the rest of the
paper relies on its ideas.
The plan of the paper is given below.
• In section 1.1, we provide a discussion of prior work and some justification as to why
– 3 –


## Page 6


we think our approach is simpler than existing ones. The main technical results of
the paper are then summarized in section 1.2.
• In section 2, we explain the web of connections between quasiequivalence and mutual
excitability, using a purely algebraic description of quantum field theory. We also
show that excitability for a generic pair of states is equivalent to excitability of their
canonical purifications.
• In section 3, we establish a few necessary criteria for excitability of Gaussian states,
which will be used throughout the rest of the paper.
• In section 4, we review the conditions under which one pure Gaussian state can be
excited out of another, using a modification of arguments from [12–14].
We also
present new proofs of theorems relating the standard “Bogoliubov” criteria to other
criteria involving the two-point functions.
• In section 5, we establish concrete formulas relating the two-point functions of Gaus-
sian states to the two-point functions of their canonical purifications. Combined with
the results of the prior section, this gives a general theorem controlling excitability
out of states for which the canonical purification is “pure” in a certain technical sense.
• In section 6, we fill a gap in the generality of our results by treating excitability for
“centrally pure” states, which are not pure in the technical sense, and which appear
when one canonically purifies a “non-factorial” state.
• In section 7, we use our prior results to show that for zero-mean Gaussian states
in free field theory, one-way excitability always implies mutual excitability; in other
words, while it is possible in general to create excitability relations like ω2 ≺ω1 with
ω1 ̸≺ω2, this cannot happen when both states are Gaussian.
• Finally, in section 8, we comment on our results, our approach, and future directions.
In addition to the pedagogical appendix A, several other appendices fill in technical ma-
terial that has been deferred to keep the main text readable. Appendix C also provides a
pedagogical introduction to one-particle modular theory.
1.1
Discussion of prior work
The main papers that inspired the present work are [1–4, 12–17]. In [12–14], criteria were
established for unitary equivalence of GNS representations coming from pure Gaussian
states. In [1], the problem of quasiequivalence for “factorial states” of free fermions was
solved using a “doubling trick” that was later connected, by [15], to what is now called
canonical purification. In [2, 3], a similar technique was applied to free bosons. The most
general theorem encompassing prior work was established by Araki and Yamagami in [4],
which relaxed the factorial assumption.1
Recently, the interesting papers [16, 17] have
1It was proved in [18] that non-factorial states do not appear in physical settings in Klein-Gordon theory,
but (i) no such result is known for generalized free field theories, and (ii) non-factorial features appear in a
– 4 –


## Page 7


revisited the Araki-Yamagami theorem in the context of modular theory; we use some
aspects of their approach in our arguments below.
This paper provides a complete proof of the general version of the Araki-Yamagami
theorem, using techniques that we believe constitute a substantial simplification over prior
approaches.2 One main advantage of our approach is that it allows us to discuss conditions
for excitability as a stepping stone towards the full question of quasiequivalence; this is
useful because it allows us to treat the possibility that ω2 can be excited out of ω1, but not
vice versa.
Below we summarize the tools that distinguish our approach from previous ones.
1.1.1
Simplifications as compared to pre-Araki-Yamagami work
Prior to the work by Araki and Yamagami, quasiequivalence was established in the factorial
setting in the papers [1–3, 12, 15]. Our approach in this setting is very closely related to
that introduced by Woronowicz in [15], but with a few improvements.
1. We use a characterization of unitary equivalence that is closer to that of Wald [13, 14]
than to that of Shale [12].
Shale’s theorem tells you how to determine whether
a particular symplectic transformation is unitarily implementable, so to apply this
theorem to the question of whether two states are unitarily equivalent, you must
first construct a symplectic transformation between the states. By contrast, Wald’s
approach is direct; the symplectic transformation is constructed “along the way” as
part of a different proof strategy.
2. We use the construction from [21] of canonical purifications on ∗-algebras to work
directly with field operators ϕ[f] when convenient, instead of having to translate
everything into the bounded “Weyl operators” eiϕ[f]. This simplifies several key argu-
ments.
3. Even though we eventually show that one-way excitability ω2 ≺ω1 always implies
quasiequivalence for Gaussian states, we find that it is conceptually cleaner to study
the conditions for one-way excitability directly, rather than seeking the conditions for
quasiequivalence from the start.
1.1.2
Simplifications as compared to Araki-Yamagami
The real power of our approach comes when simplifying and generalizing the theorem of
Araki and Yamagami [4]. Our progress results from the following improvements.
state-independent way in theories with higher-form symmetries. It therefore seems worthwhile to deal with
the non-factorial case here.
2It is very difficult to quantify “simplicity,” so a statement like this one is necessarily ideological. After
all, the present paper is over a hundred pages long including appendices! On the other hand, the present
paper is mostly self-contained, and is written in a pedagogical “physics style” as opposed to a more concise
“mathematical style.” The Araki-Yamagami paper [4] is 56 math-pages long and depends on both conceptual
and technical results from the prior papers [2, 12, 19, 20].
– 5 –


## Page 8


1. Following Woronowicz [15], we obtain the “doubling trick” used in prior work as a
special case of a general canonical purification formula [21]. We extend the validity
of Woronowicz’s approach by applying canonical purifications to non-factorial states
as in [21].
2. Since the canonical purifications of general states are “centrally pure,” we tackle the
question of excitability for general states by passing to the canonical purification, then
determining the conditions under which centrally pure states can be excited out of
one another. There is a sense in which any centrally pure state breaks up into a “pure
piece” and an “abelian piece,” and we decompose the excitability problem into these
two components.
3. We produce a version of Wald’s characterization of unitary equivalence that applies
to the “abelian” excitability problem just mentioned. This allows us to avoid invoking
Segal’s lemma on absolute continuity of Gaussian random processes [19], so that no
part of the present work is dependent on results from constructive quantum field
theory.
1.2
Summary of results
The details of the paper are rather technical, so we summarize here all essential results
that may be useful in future applications. We write this section, and only this section,
in definition-theorem-proof format, where after each theorem we point to the place in the
paper where the proof can be found. Results are presented in the order in which they are
proved, with dependencies between theorems pointed out, so that the logical structure of
the arguments is clear.

Remark 1.3. If A0 is a C∗algebra and ω : A0 →C is norm-continuous, then for every

In this setting, the excitability relation ω2 ≺ω1 is equivalent to ultraweak continuity of the
“identity map” that takes the representative of a on Hω1 to the representative of a on Hω2.
Definition 1.4. A state ω : A0 →C is said to be factorial if Aω is a factor.
Proposition 1.5. If ω1 is factorial and we have ω2 ≺ω1, then we automatically have
ω1 ≺ω2.
Proof. See section 2.2.
Proposition 1.6. If ω1 is not factorial, then there always exists a state ω2 with ω2 ≺ω1
but ω1 ̸≺ω2.
Proof. See section 2.2.
Proposition 1.7. If ω2 ≺ω1 and ω1 ≺ω2, then the “field-preserving map” α from propo-
sition 1.2 is a normal isomorphism from Aω1 onto Aω2. In this case we write ω1 ∼ω2 and
say the two states are quasiequivalent.
Proof. See section 2.1.

Proof. The canonical purification of a factorial state is pure, so we may combine theorems

Theorem 1.27. If ω1 is pure, then in the set of conditions for ω2 ≺ω1, the assumption
that √X2 −√X1 is Hilbert-Schmidt is equivalent to the condition that the diagonal elements
of X2 −X1, within the block decomposition for R1, be trace class.
Equivalently, one may check that Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt and that Q + R1Q−1R1 is
trace-class.
(Note that R1 has eigenvalues ±i in this setting; these are the eigenvalues with respect
to which the block decomposition is taken.)
Proof. See section 5.5.
Proposition 1.28. There exist examples where ω1 is pure, ω2 is mixed, the block-diagonal
elements of X2 −X1 are trace class, but X2 −X1 is not itself trace-class.
Proof. See appendix F.2.
Theorem 1.29. Given a free field algebra with Ω= 0, i.e., an abelian free field algebra,
one has ω2 ≺ω1 if and only if
(i) µ2 ≺µ1,
(ii) Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt, and
(iii) Q has trivial kernel.
Proof. See section 6.3.
Theorem 1.30. When ω1 and ω2 are centrally pure, the excitability relation ω2 ≺ω1 is
equivalent to
(i) µ2 ≺µ1,
(ii) Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt, and
(iii) Q has trivial kernel.
– 11 –


## Page 14


Proof. This is proved in section 6 by breaking a centrally pure state into a “pure piece”
and an “abelian piece” (section 6.2), and then combining theorems 1.27 and 1.29 (section
6.4).
Theorem 1.31. For zero-mean Gaussian states ω1 and ω2, the excitability relation ω2 ≺ω1
is equivalent to
(i) µ2 ≺µ1,
(ii) √X2 −√X1 is Hilbert-Schmidt, and
(iii) Q has trivial kernel.
Proof. This follows immediately by combining theorem 1.30 with theorem 1.24.
Theorem 1.32. For zero-mean Gaussian states, ω2 ≺ω1 always implies ω1 ≺ω2.
Proof. This theorem is proved in section 7 from the necessary conditions (i-iii) of theorem
1.31.
Remark 1.33. By combining this last theorem with proposition 1.7, we learn that for
quasiequivalence, it is necessary and sufficient to check the conditions of theorem 1.31. This
should be contrasted with the work of Araki and Yamagami [4], in which quasiequivalence is
shown to be equivalent to conditions (i) and (ii) of theorem 1.31, together with the condition
µ1 ≺µ2.
To see consistency of our results with those of Araki and Yamagami, note that once one
has µ2 ≺µ1 and µ1 ≺µ2, then it is obvious that Q must be invertible, so it cannot have a
kernel — this gives condition (iii) in our theorem. On the other hand, as explained in section
5, condition (ii) of our theorem implies that Q is invertible away from its kernel, so when
condition (iii) is satisfied, we learn that Q is invertible, which gives the Araki-Yamagami
condition µ1 ≺µ2.
2
Quasiequivalence and excitability
In the introduction, we alluded to the problem of “quasiequivalence,” which has its roots
in [22] and was explored extensively in [1–5, 15]. This is the question of when two sectors
of the same quantum field theory have physically isomorphic von Neumann algebras. The
goals of the present section are: (i) to explain the connection between quasiequivalence
and “mutual excitability;” (ii) to establish weaker algebraic criteria that are equivalent
to “one-way excitability;” (iii) to demonstrate that one-way excitability of generic states
is equivalent to one-way excitability of the canonical purifications; and (iv) to accomplish
points (i)-(iii) in a framework that is appropriate for general interacting field theories, rather
than the C∗-algebraic framework of [15] that is only appropriate for free theories.
While later sections of this paper are specifically about free field theory, this section is
written in the fashion of [21], where a ∗-algebraic framework is used to encourage future
generalizations beyond the setting of free fields.
– 12 –


## Page 15


2.1
Quasiequivalence and mutual excitability
In [15, 22], the problem of quasiequivalence is formulated as follows. One has an abstract
C∗-algebra A0 of quantum fields, on which one specifies a continuous quantum state ω
and constructs the GNS representation Hω. Within this representation, the C∗-algebra A0
can be completed to a von Neumann algebra Aω. Two states ω1 and ω2 are said to be
“quasiequivalent” if there is a ∗-isomorphism between Aω1 and Aω2 that acts as the identity
on A0. We will begin by explaining the equivalence of this criterion to “mutual excitability,”
then generalize to the setting where we may only assume that A0 is a ∗-algebra.
As explained in the introduction, it is natural to say that the state ω2 can be “excited”
out of ω1 if there is a density matrix on Hω1 that reproduces all of the ω2 correlation
functions. Concretely, we say “ω2 can be excited out of ω1” if there is a density matrix ρω2
on Hω1 satisfying4
trHω1(ρω2a) = ω2(a)
∀a ∈A0.
(2.1)
We write this excitability relation as ω2 ≺ω1.
Suppose now that the states ω1 and ω2 are quasiequivalent. This means there is a
∗-isomorphism α : Aω1 →Aω2 that acts as the identity on A0. In this case, one can make
use of a foundational theorem about von Neumann algebras — see e.g. [24, corollary 5.13]
— which says that every ∗-isomorphism is automatically continuous with respect to the
ultraweak topology.5 Since ω2 is automatically ultraweakly continuous as a functional on
Aω2, the pullback ω2◦α is ultraweakly continuous as a functional on Aω1. Moreover, because
α preserves A0, one has
(ω2 ◦α)(a) = ω2(a)
∀a ∈A0.
(2.2)
This means that there is an ultraweakly continuous functional on Aω1 that reproduces
the correlation functions of ω2. It is a basic fact about von Neumann algebras — see
e.g.
[26, theorem 46.4] — that every positive, ultraweakly continuous functional has a
density matrix representative.
Putting these pieces together, we see that when ω1 and
ω2 are quasiequivalent, we have the excitability relation ω2 ≺ω1. In fact, because α is a
∗-isomorphism, it is invertible, and we can run the argument the other way — this implies
that when ω1 and ω2 are quasiequivalent, we have ω2 ≺ω1 and ω1 ≺ω2, i.e., we have
mutual excitability.
In fact, it turns out that mutual excitability is the same statement as quasiequivalence.
That is, if we assume ω2 ≺ω1 and ω1 ≺ω2, then we may conclude that there is a ∗-
isomorphism from Aω1 to Aω2 that preserves A0. First, beginning with the assumption
ω2 ≺ω1, one can go to “standard form” for Aω1 by selecting a particular faithful functional
ϕ1 : Aω1 →C and going to its GNS representation Hϕ1. A review of this procedure can
4We note that this density matrix representative will not generally live in Aω1 itself — in fact this is
impossible if Aω1 is not type I (see [23]).
5A review of the ultraweak topology can be found in [25, section 2.1], but the basic idea is that in the
ultraweak topology on a von Neumann algebra A, a sequence (or net) of operators an ∈A converges to an
operator a ∈A if and only if we have
tr(ρan) →tr(ρa)
for every density matrix ρ acting on H.
– 13 –


## Page 16


be found in [21, section 4.1]; the important point is that one has Aω1 ∼= Aϕ1, i.e., no new
operators are introduced in the GNS representation Hϕ1. Since we have assumed ω2 ≺
ω1, there is a natural extension of ω2 to an ultraweakly continuous functional on Aω1,
and hence on Aϕ1. Moreover, because |ϕ1⟩is cyclic and separating for Aϕ1 within Hϕ1,
every ultraweakly continuous functional on Aϕ1 has a vector representative — see e.g. [24,
corollary 5.24]. So there is a representative |ω2⟩ϕ1 ∈Hϕ1. Defining the operator U : Hϕ1 →
Hω2 by
Ua|ω2⟩ϕ1 = a|ω2⟩ω2,
a ∈A0,
(2.3)
and defining U to vanish on the orthocomplement of the set A0|ω2⟩ϕ1, one easily sees that
U is a partial isometry. One also easily sees that U conjugates elements of A0 acting on
Hϕ1 to the same elements of A0 acting on Hω2. So conjugation by U serves as the “identity
map” on A0 from Aω1 to Aω2, and conjugation by a partial isometry is always ultraweakly
continuous. One therefore concludes, from the assumption ω2 ≺ω1, that the identity map
from A0 ⊆Aω1 to A0 ⊆Aω2 is ultraweakly continuous. Repeating the same argument for
the assumption ω1 ≺ω2, one forms a chain of ultraweakly continuous maps
Aω1 →Aω2 →Aω1
(2.4)
that compose to the identity.
It follows that the “identity map” on A0 provides a ∗-
isomorphism from Aω1 to Aω2.
Thus far we have established that “quasiequivalence” and “mutual excitability” are iden-
tical notions for the C∗-algebraic framework considered in [15, 22]. However, this framework
is not believed to be appropriate for general quantum field theories [27, 28]. In free field
theories, because the commutator of two fields is proportional to the identity, the Baker-
Campbell-Hausdorff formula allows one to create a C∗-algebra of “exponentiated fields”
eiϕ[f] [29, 30]. For a general field theory, however, we can only assume that we have a ∗-
algebra generated by smeared fields ϕ[f], and they cannot be exponentiated in an abstract
way that is independent of representation.
To generalize the above discussion, we now assume that A0 is only an abstract ∗-
algebra. As reviewed in [21, appendix A.3], any algebraic state ω : A0 →C gives rise to a
GNS representation Hω on which the elements of A0 act as closed, unbounded operators.
We follow the choice made in [21], where Aω is taken to be the smallest von Neumann
algebra on Hω such that all elements of A0 are “affiliated.” Equivalently, as proved in [21,
appendix A.4], one may take arbitrary polar decompositions of elements in A0,
a = ua|a|,
(2.5)
and take Aω to be generated by all partial isometries ua, their adjoints u†

functions f(|a|) of the self-adjoint operators |a|.
In this setting, what should we mean when we say that ω1 is quasiequivalent to ω2?
We want to say that this happens whenever there is a ∗-isomorphism from Aω1 to Aω2 that
“preserves the field algebra A0.” But because A0 acts in an unbounded way on each space
Hωj, it is not a subalgebra of either von Neumann algebra Aωj. Instead, it makes sense
to say that a ∗-isomorphism α : Aω1 →Aω2 is a quasiequivalence if it preserves the polar

By a similar logic to the C∗-algebra case, it is obvious that quasiequivalence of ω1 and
ω2 implies mutual excitability. The converse is slightly more subtle. As in the C∗-algebra
case, one begins with the assumption ω2 ≺ω1 and passes to standard form for Aω1 by going
to an equivalent von Neumann algebra Aϕ1 acting on Hϕ1. In the C∗-algebraic case, this
isomorphism would map A0 ⊆Aω1 to an equivalent subalgebra of Aϕ1. In the general, ∗-
algebraic case, we must deal with the fact that A0 is not a subalgebra of Aω1. Nevertheless,
the unbounded operators in A0 actually do map, in a natural way, into operators acting on
Hϕ1. This map, constructed in [21, section 4.1], uses the polar decomposition explicitly. As
a consequence, the polar decompositions of elements of A0 acting on Hω1 are canonically
isomorphic, within Aω1, to the polar decompositions acting on Hϕ1 within Aϕ1. One can
then proceed as in the C∗algebraic case to construct a map U that conjugates polar
decompositions of A0 elements on Aω1 to the corresponding polar decompositions of A0
elements on Aω2. This map is ultraweakly continuous, and repeating the argument for
ω1 ≺ω2 provides an inverse, so the conjugation map is a quasiequivalence isomorphism, as
desired.
2.2
One-way excitability and normal homomorphisms
We have shown that algebraic quasiequivalence is the same as the mutual excitability rela-
tion ω2 ≺ω1 and ω1 ≺ω2. Here, we show the following weakened statements:
• We have ω2 ≺ω1 if and only if there exists an ultraweakly continuous ∗-homomorphism
from Aω1 to Aω2 that acts as the identity on field operators.6 An ultraweakly con-
tinuous ∗-homomorphism is called a “normal homomorphism,” and we will use this
terminology below.7
6“Acting as the identity” is meant in the “polar decomposition sense” of the previous subsection.
7Let us remark that any sensible definition of excitability should be transitive; that is, ω3 ≺ω2 and
ω2 ≺ω1 should imply ω3 ≺ω1. By recasting excitability in terms of normal homomorphisms, this tran-
sitivity property becomes automatic; clearly the maps Aω1 →Aω2 and Aω2 →Aω3 compose to a normal
homomorphism Aω1 →Aω3.
– 15 –


## Page 18


• If we have ω2 ≺ω1, and Aω1 is a factor, then we automatically have mutual excitability
ω1 ≺ω2. Conversely, if Aω1 is not a factor, then there always exists a state ω2 that
can be excited out of ω1 but not vice versa.
The proof of the first statement is almost trivial given what we already know. When we
proved that having a quasiequivalence map from Aω1 to Aω2 induces the relation ω2 ≺ω1,
we actually did not use the assumption that the homomorphism was invertible; we only
used its ultraweak continuity and the fact that it preserves the field algebra. The converse
relation, ω1 ≺ω2, was the only part of the argument that used the invertibility assumption.
So by repeating the logic of the previous subsection, one finds that whenever we have a
field-preserving normal homomorphism from Aω1 to Aω2, we have ω2 ≺ω1.
Conversely, given ω2 ≺ω1, and using the trick of passing to standard form, we already
showed in the preceding subsection that one obtains a normal homomorphism from Aω1
to Aω2 that preserves polar decompositions; the converse assumption ω1 ≺ω2 was only
needed if we wanted to show that this normal homomorphism had an inverse.

2.3
Checking excitability with canonical purifications
In the rest of the paper, we will be concerned with establishing concrete conditions for
ω2 ≺ω1 when ω1 and ω2 are Gaussian states of a free field theory. The technique we will
use is to begin by studying the case where ω1 and ω2 are pure, then studying more general
states by using canonical purifications.
This technique goes back to [1] for the specific
case of “factorial states” in free fermion theories. A general framework was worked out by
Woronowicz in [15] for factorial states on C∗algebras. Here we generalize Woronowicz’s
result away from factorial states, and away from C∗algebras.
In particular, we consider a general ∗-algebra A0 and an algebraic state ω : A0 →C.
In [21], the canonical purification of ω was defined as a particular algebraic state bω :

in [21]. This means that the von Neumann algebra (A0 ⊗Aop

3
Some necessary conditions for Gaussian states
So far we have been looking at conditions for excitability for general states of general
quantum theories. Here, we establish some necessary conditions for excitability of zero-
mean Gaussian states in free theories. These conditions will form the basis of the detailed
analysis of the rest of the paper.
– 19 –


## Page 22


3.1
Boundedness of the symmetrized inner product
Suppose that ω1 and ω2 are zero-mean Gaussian states, and suppose that there is a density
matrix ρω2 in the GNS space Hω1 that reproduces the correlation functions of ω2 on the
Weyl algebra. The existence of this density matrix turns out to imply a useful relationship
between the symmetrized inner products µ1 and µ2 that were defined in appendix A.5.
Recall, from that appendix, that one has
⟨f|g⟩µ1 = ω1(ϕ[f∗]ϕ[g] + ϕ[g]ϕ[f∗])
2
,
(3.1)
that the Hilbert space of test functions endowed with this inner product is called Kµ1, and
that one defines an operator R1 that implements the commutator,
⟨f|R1|g⟩µ1 = 1
2Ω[f∗, g].
(3.2)
As explained in appendix A.6, the map from test functions to Weyl operators, f 7→eiϕ[f],
is continuous with respect to the µ1 topology on the domain and the strong-Hω1 topology
on the image.10 Since the image of this map is in the set of unitary operators, which all
have norm one, this map is actually continuous in the ultrastrong topology — it is a basic
fact that the strong and ultrastrong topologies agree on bounded sets. Finally, because
the ultraweak topology is weaker than the ultrastrong topology, one may conclude that the
map f 7→eiϕ[f] is continuous when one uses the ultraweak topology in the image space.
Consequently, if one has a sequence of real test functions fn that converges to zero with
respect to µ1, then one has
trHω1(ρω2eiϕ[fn]) →1.
(3.3)
Expanding eiϕ[fn] as a power series, one finds
e−⟨fn|fn⟩µ2/2 →1,
(3.4)
hence ⟨fn|fn⟩µ2 →0.
So given any real sequence fn with ⟨fn|fn⟩µ1 →0, one has ⟨fn|fn⟩µ2 →0. Since one
has ⟨u+iv|u+iv⟩µ1 = ⟨u|u⟩µ1 +⟨v|v⟩µ1 for u and v real, one can reach the same conclusion
for general complex fn. It follows that the inner product µ2 is bounded by a multiple of the
inner product µ1, i.e., there exists some number c with
⟨f|f⟩µ2 ≤c ⟨f|f⟩µ1
(3.5)
for any test function f. One writes this relation as µ2 ≺µ1, and says that µ2 is “dominated
by µ1.” Note that in the special case where ω1 is factorial, meaning that Aω1 is a factor, we
showed in section 2.2 that ω2 ≺ω1 automatically implies ω1 ≺ω2. So under the assumption
ω2 ≺ω1, we automatically have that µ1 is bounded by a multiple of µ2:
⟨f|f⟩µ1 ≤c′ ⟨f|f⟩µ2.
(3.6)
10A quick introduction to operator topologies can be found in [25, section 2.1].

## Page 23


In particular, this will be the case when ω1 is pure, since then we have Aω1 = B(Hω1),
which is a factor.
Whenever we have µ2 ≺µ1, we can define a bounded map L : Kµ1 →Kµ2 that acts on
test functions as
L|f⟩µ1 = |f⟩µ2,
(3.7)
so that the image of L is dense in Kµ2. This map L will be used many times in the rest of
the paper, as it is the only tool we have for moving between the spaces Kµ1 and Kµ2. In
particular, while L acts “as the identity” on test functions, other elements of Kµ1 cannot be
identified naturally with elements of Kµ2; they can only be mapped to Kµ2 using L.
One advantage of having introduced L is that it lets us formulate excitability using
the “completed spaces” Kµ1 and Kµ2 instead of the more restrictive space of test functions.
Recall that, by definition, we said that we can excite ω2 out of ω1 if for some density matrix
ρω2 in Hω1 we have
trHω1(ρω2eiϕ[f]) = ⟨ω2|eiϕ[f]|ω2⟩
(3.8)
for all test functions f. Using the continuity of the map from real test functions to Weyl
operators on Hωj, one can define a Weyl operator eiϕ[ψ] acting on Hωj for any real element of
Kµj — see appendix A for details. Making use of continuity, one can equivalently formulate
excitability as the requirement that for any real |ψ⟩in Kµ1, one has
trHω1(ρω2eiϕ[ψ]) = ⟨ω2|eiϕ[Lψ]|ω2⟩.
(3.9)
This notation will appear many times in what follows.
Note that while L is bounded whenever we have µ2 ≺µ1, nothing we have discussed
so far implies that it is invertible. Ultimately, we will actually see that invertibility of L
does follow from the excitability relation ω2 ≺ω1, but this will take some doing and relies
heavily on results in the rest of the paper. In the next subsection, we will show at least
that when one has ω2 ≺ω1, the map L cannot have a kernel.
Before proceeding to that result, we demonstrate a very useful identity relating L to the
operators R1 and R2 that implement the commutator on Kµ1 and Kµ2, which are defined
via the identity
⟨f|Rj|g⟩µj = 1

(3.10)

3.2
No kernel for L
So far, we know that when we have ω2 ≺ω1, the identity map L|f⟩µ1 = |f⟩µ2 is bounded.
We will now show that when excitability holds, L cannot have a kernel. This is a striking
property of Gaussian states that has no analogue for general states in general quantum
field theories, and it will be used in section 7 — after developing the rest of the paper’s
machinery — to rule out the possibility of having ω2 ≺ω1 while having ω1 ̸≺ω2.
First, assume that there is a state |ψ⟩µ1 in the kernel of L; without loss of generality,
we will take |ψ⟩µ1 to be normalized. Since L commutes with complex conjugation, we may
as well take |ψ⟩µ1 to be fixed by the antiunitary complex conjugation map Γ on Kµ1. For
any real number t, we have
trHω1(ρω2eitϕ[ψ]) = ⟨ω2|eitϕ[Lψ]|ω2⟩= 1.
(3.14)
This implies that the support of ρω2 lies within the +1 eigenspace of the unitary operator
eitϕ[ψ]. For if we diagonalize the density matrix as ρω2 = P

To summarize, with the work of this section, we have proven part of the “necessary”
direction of our main theorem, 1.31. In particular, that theorem claims that the general
conditions for excitability are (i) µ2 ≺µ1, (ii) √X2 −√X1 Hilbert-Schmidt, and (iii)
Q ≡L†L has no kernel. While we have not yet discussed condition (ii), we have already
shown that (i) and (iii) are necessary conditions.
4
Gaussian pure-state excitability
Given a free field algebra A0, a state ω : A0 →C is said to be pure if, in the GNS
representation Hω, the von Neumann algebra Aω has trivial commutant; that is, Aω is the
full set B(Hω) of all bounded operators on Hω. In this section, we address the question of
excitability for two zero-mean Gaussian states ω1 and ω2 that are both pure.
This section is essentially a review of the result from [12], following the arguments
due to Wald in [13, 14]. We make certain modifications to the arguments that allow us to
generalize, in the following section, to centrally pure states; also, in section 4.6, we present
a new argument for the equivalence of the conditions from [12–14] with the conditions from
Araki and Yamagami in [4].
We begin by assuming ω2 ≺ω1 and deriving a set of necessary conditions; then, at the
end of the section, we will turn the argument around and show that these conditions are
sufficient.
11For example, Stirling’s approximation shows that (2m!)/((2m!!)2) scales like 1/√m for large m.
– 23 –


## Page 26


4.1
Vectors versus density matrices
In principle, our assumptions in this section should be that ω1 and ω2 are pure states on
the free field algebra (which can be taken to be the C∗algebra of Weyl operators), and that
there exists a density matrix ρω2 on Hω1 that reproduces the correlation functions of ω2. In
this case, however, it turns out that whenever ρω2 exists, it must actually be rank 1, and
so going forward we can write ρω2 = |ω2⟩⟨ω2|, with |ω2⟩a vector representative for ω2 in
Hω1.12
To see this, suppose that ρω2 is a nontrivial density matrix, so that we can write it as
a convex combination of other density matrices on Hω1:
ρω2 = pσ + (1 −p)τ.
(4.1)
Each of the density matrices σ and τ defines a state on the von Neumann algebra Aω1, and
induces a state on the C∗subalgebra of Weyl operators. This allows us to decompose ω2 as
a convex combination of two states on the C∗algebra, but by purity of ω2, these two states
must be equal. So σ and τ have the same correlation functions on the full Weyl subalgebra,
and since the Weyl subalgebra is ultraweakly dense in Aω1, we may conclude that σ and
τ reproduce the same correlation functions on all of Aω1. Since Aω1 is the full algebra of
bounded operators B(Hω1), this implies that σ and τ are equal as density matrices, which
is a contradiction.

This basic approach was developed in [13, 14].

4.5
Sufficiency
So far we have determined that when ω1 and ω2 are both pure, and there is a representative
for ω2 in Hω1, then (i) the inner product µ2 must be bounded by the inner product µ1;
and (ii) the operator Π1,iL−1Π2,−i must be Hilbert-Schmidt as a map from Kµ2 to Kµ1.
This last condition, while it sounds technical, is really just the normalizability condition

For m = 2, using similar logic, our desired identity (with f1 = f and f2 = g) simplifies to

But we can freely replace each pair of fields in the above expression by its commutator,

⟨ΓL−1f−i|R1|L−1gi⟩µ1 = ⟨Γf−i|R2|gi⟩µ2.

But this equality follows from the identity R1 = L†R2L, which we verified in equation

We can now proceed by induction. For odd m, our inductive assumption will be that
the k-point correlators vanish for odd k < m. This allows us to commute terms freely, since
replacing two fields by their commutator brings us to an odd-point function of lower degree,
which vanishes by assumption. So we can freely commute all of the fk,−i fields to the right
and all of the fk,i fields to the left, leading to zero on both sides of equation (4.39) in the
case of odd m. For the even-point correlators, the identity we want to show is
⟨ω2|ϕ[L−1f1,−i] . . . ϕ[L−1fm,i]|ω2⟩1 = ⟨ω2|ϕ[f1,−i] . . . ϕ[fm,i]|ω2⟩2.
(4.43)
On both sides of the equation, we can commute the ϕ[fm,i] through other fields at the cost
of introducing a commutator, but each of these is a product of a commutator (which agree
on both sides as in equation (4.42)) and an even-point function of lower degree (which agree
on both sides by the inductive assumption). So we only need to worry about the term where
the fm,i field is moved all the way to the front of both expressions — but these terms both
vanish, since this field annihilates both bras ⟨ω2|1 and ⟨ω2|2.
4.6
Connection to the two-point function
In this section we have established, following [13, 14], that when ω1 and ω2 are both pure,
then ω2 has a representative in Hω1 if and only if (i) the symmetrized inner product µ2 is
bounded by the symmetrized inner product µ1, and (ii) within the space Kµ1, the operator
Π1,iL−1Π2,−i is Hilbert-Schmidt. To make contact with the theorem we will prove in the
general case of a mixed state, it is helpful to rewrite condition (ii) in terms of information
about the two-point functions induced by ω1 and ω2.

To see that the first condition implies the second, we will make our first use of a technique
that will be employed many times in the remainder of the paper, which is Birman-Solomyak
theory. The basic idea of Birman Solomyak theory is to tell you when, given a function
f and a pair of positive operators A and B, one conclude that f(A) −f(B) is in the p-
Schatten-class whenever A−B is in that class. The key theory was developed by Birman and
Solomyak in the 1960s; see [32] for a comprehensive textbook account. For our purposes,
we only need the Hilbert-Schmidt version of the theory, for which one can find nice, simple
proofs in [33].
This is the form of the theorem we will use, though it admits generalizations:
Theorem 4.1 (Birman-Solomyak). Let A and B be bounded, self-adjoint operators on a
Hilbert space H. Suppose that f : R →R is Lipschitz on the union of the spectra of A and
B. Then whenever A −B is Hilbert-Schmidt, so is f(A) −f(B).
A function is said to be “Lipschitz” on a domain if all of its tangent lines have uniformly
bounded slope. Concretely, if one has

## Page 37


5
Gaussian excitability via canonical purifications
In the preceding section, we proved theorem 1.18 — that for zero-mean Gaussian states,
when ω1 and ω2 are both pure, the necessary and sufficient criteria for ω2 ≺ω1 are (i)
µ2 ≺µ1 and (ii) Q −1 Hilbert-Schmidt.
We also showed that in this case, µ2 ≺µ1
automatically implies µ1 ≺µ2, so one does not need to check that result separately.
The main theorem of the paper, 1.31, claims that the general conditions for excitability
are (i) µ2 ≺µ1, (ii) √X2 −√X1 Hilbert-Schmidt, and (iii) Q has no kernel. To prepare for
proving this general theorem, this section provides a proof of theorem 1.24, which establishes
equivalence of these conditions with certain conditions on the canonical purification.
Namely, we prove the equivalence of the following conditions:
• µ2 ≺µ1, and √X2 −√X1 Hilbert-Schmidt, and Q has no kernel;
• bµ2 ≺bµ1, and bQ −1 Hilbert-Schmidt, and bQ has no kernel.
In fact, we remark that the equivalence of the other conditions could be proved without
making any assumptions about the kernels of Q or bQ, since the Hilbert-Schmidt conditions
imply that Q and bQ have finite-dimensional kernels, and these finite-dimensional kernels
can be treated separately without any real complications. But on physical grounds there
is no need to prove this more general statement, since we established in section 3 that the
absence of a kernel is a necessary condition for excitability.
At the end of the section, we explain how our results imply theorem 1.26, which states
that when ω1 is a factor state, the excitability statement ω2 ≺ω1 is equivalent to the
statements µ2 ≺µ1 with √X2−√X1 Hilbert-Schmidt. While this may seem a little puzzling
— as no explicit mention is made in theorem 1.26 of the necessary condition that Q have
no kernel — we explain in section 5.4 why the conditions of theorem 1.26 automatically
imply that Q has no kernel, without needing to check this condition separately.
5.1
Canonical purifications of Gaussian states
We begin by discussing general properties of the canonical purification bω of a Gaussian
state ω. In particular, we explain that the canonical purification of a Gaussian state is
itself Gaussian.

## Page 41


If µ2 and µ1 are mutually dominated by one another, then L is an invertible operator,
and one can conclude from this equation that one has |R2||fn −gn⟩µ2 →0. This is where
the assumptions on Q and √X2 −√X1 come in. Since √X2 −√X1 is Hilbert-Schmidt, by
multiplying on either side by √X2+√X1 and taking linear combinations, one may conclude
that the operator X2 −X1 = Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt. Since Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt,
the operator Q must have a finite number of eigenvalues in any subset of the real line that
is bounded away from one. In particular, Q must have a finite kernel, and there must be
a gap between its kernel and its first nonzero eigenvalue. When we put in the additional
assumption that Q has no kernel, we learn that Q is an invertible map; since Q is defined
by Q = L†L, we conclude that L is invertible. So from the convergence of equation (5.26),
we may conclude
⟨R2(fn −gn)|R2(fn −gn)⟩µ2 →0,
(5.27)
as desired.

5.3.2
First Hilbert-Schmidt conditions
Putting all this together, we have demonstrated that equations (5.44) and (5.45) tell us
everything we need to know about bQ by breaking it into two pieces bQsym and bQantisym.
In particular, bQ −1 will be Hilbert-Schmidt if and only if bQsym −1 and bQantisym −1 are
Hilbert-Schmidt. So instead of checking that bQ −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt on Kbµ1, we may
equivalently check that
•
1

1

•
1
√1−W1 (Q −1 −W2 + W1)
1
√1−W1 is Hilbert-Schmidt on the support of R1.
Since the operator √1 + W1 is invertible, we may ignore it in the first expression, making
our conditions
• Q −1 + W2 −W1 is Hilbert-Schmidt on Kµ1, and
•
1
√1−W1 (Q −1 −W2 + W1)
1
√1−W1 is Hilbert-Schmidt on the support of R1.
Even though
1
√1−W1 is not bounded, we still know that multiplying a Hilbert-Schmidt
operator on the left and right by √1 −W1 produces a Hilbert-Schmidt operator. From this
we can conclude that Hilbert-Schmidtness of bQ −1 implies the conditions
• (Q −1) + (W2 −W1) is Hilbert-Schmidt on Kµ1, and
• (Q −1) −(W2 −W1) is Hilbert-Schmidt on the support of R1.
A simple calculation shows that (Q −1) −(W2 −W1) actually vanishes on the kernel of
R1,20 so the second condition trivially extends to all of Kµ1. Overall, we simply find that
the Hilbert-Schmidt property for bQ −1 implies the conditions
• Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt on Kµ1, and
• W2 −W1 is Hilbert-Schmidt on Kµ1.
These two conditions are necessary for bQ−1 to be Hilbert-Schmidt. Next we will show
that these conditions are actually sufficient; then in the following subsection, we will show
that they are equivalent to the single condition that √X2 −√X1 be Hilbert-Schmidt, which
finally proves theorem 1.24.

5.4
Consequences for factorial states
Now we have proven theorem 1.24. In practice, this theorem will be used to reduce the
problem of excitability for general mixed states, to the simpler problem of excitability for
purified states. Indeed, with theorem 1.24 in hand, we may now prove theorem 1.26, which
addresses mixed-state excitability ω2 ≺ω1 subject to the assumption that ω1 is a factorial
state. Relaxing the factorial assumption is the task of section 6.
Theorem 1.26 says that if ω1 is factorial, then we have ω2 ≺ω1 if and only if we have
µ2 ≺µ1 and √X2 −√X1 is Hilbert-Schmidt. Notice that there is no condition checking
that Q is kernel-free. The reason is that whenever we have µ2 ≺µ1, we may use the identity
L†R2L = R1 from equation (3.12). This equation implies that anything in the kernel of L
must also be in the kernel of R1. So when ω1 is factorial, meaning R1 has trivial kernel, we
may conclude that L and Q = L†L have trivial kernel. So when ω1 is factorial, the triviality
of the kernel of Q does not need to be checked separately from the condition µ2 ≺µ1.
Now we prove the direction of theorem 1.26 that starts with the assumption ω2 ≺ω1.
Since ω1 is factorial, proposition 1.5 tells us that we automatically have ω1 ≺ω2. This leads
to an isomorphism of the von Neumann algebras Aω1 and Aω2, so ω2 is factorial as well.
Since the canonical purification of a factorial state is pure, this means that both bω1 and bω2
are pure. The general theorem 1.11 tells us that ω2 ≺ω1 implies bω2 ≺bω1, and since both
– 47 –


## Page 50


of these states are pure, we learn from theorem 1.18 that we must have bµ2 ≺bµ1, with bQ−1
Hilbert-Schmidt. Since bω1 is pure we automatically get ker bQ = 0, and theorem 1.24 then
tells us that we have µ2 ≺µ1, and √X2 −√X1 Hilbert-Schmidt.
In the other direction, assume that ω1 is factorial, and that we have µ2 ≺µ1, together
with √X2 −√X1 Hilbert-Schmidt. As discussed above, ker Q is automatically trivial, so
theorem 1.24 gives bµ2 ≺bµ1, with bQ −1 Hilbert-Schmidt, and with the kernel of bQ trivial.
We want to use these facts to reach the conclusion bω2 ≺bω1, from which we can conclude
ω2 ≺ω1 by theorem 1.11.
If we knew that bω1 and bω2 were both pure, then we could apply theorem 1.18, and we
would be done. But so far, we only know that ω1 is factorial, so that bω1 is pure — we don’t
know anything about bω2. Let us show that the assumptions we are making actually imply
that bω2 is pure.
Since we know that bQ −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt and ker bQ is trivial, we know that bQ is
invertible,21 so we may conclude that bL is invertible. Using the identity bL† bR2bL = bR1, we
conclude that bR2 has trivial kernel, since if we had
bR2|ψ⟩= 0,

but this implies |ψ⟩= 0, since bR1 has trivial kernel. So bR2 has trivial kernel, and Abω2 has
trivial center. Since bω2 is centrally pure, we may conclude from the fact that it is factorial
that bω2 must be genuinely pure. This completes the proof, since now we may apply theorem
1.18 to conclude bω2 ≺bω1, hence ω2 ≺ω1.

6
Centrally pure Gaussian states
So far, we have proved theorem 1.26. This gives a general criterion for excitability ω2 ≺ω1
when ω1 is a factorial state. The key insight was that when ω1 is factorial, the canonical
purification bω1 is pure, so as explained in section 5.4, one can apply the theorem that says
pure states can be excited out of one another if and only if one has bµ2 ≺bµ1 and the operator
bQ −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt.
For a non-factorial state, the canonical purification is not pure in the technical sense;
instead, it is only centrally pure. Crucially, we can still prove the paper’s main theorem
— theorem 1.31 — by using the simple logic of section 5.4, as long as the excitability
conditions for centrally pure states mimic those of pure states. So to complete the proof,
we will establish the appropriate excitability criteria for centrally pure states.
In this section, we will take ω1 and ω2 to be centrally pure states.25 As in remark 1.10,
a centrally pure state ω is characterized by the inclusion A′
ω ⊆Aω, which expresses the
absence of new “purifying” degrees of freedom introduced to A0 by the commutant in the
GNS representation Hω. In the particular case of centrally pure Gaussian states, central
purity is equivalent to the statement that R1 and R2 have spectra ±i and zero. We will
prove theorem 1.30, which states that one has ω2 ≺ω1 if and only if µ2 ≺µ1, ker Q = 0,
and the operator Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt.
The idea of the proof is to split ω1 into a “pure piece” and an “abelian piece.”
In
particular, while the ±i eigenspaces of R1 generate a subsystem for which ω1 is pure (in
the strict, factorial sense), the kernel of R1 generates a subsystem for which ω1 is classical
(in the sense of describing a state on an abelian algebra). One can then break the criterion
ω2 ≺ω1 into these two pieces, and apply theorem 1.27 — which tells us when a general
mixed state can be excited from a pure state — together with a new theorem that will tell
us when excitability is possible on an abelian algebra. Putting these together will yield a
proof of theorem 1.31.
6.1
Structure of centrally pure states
We begin by explaining how a centrally pure Gaussian state ω can be split into a “pure
piece” and an “abelian piece” by appealing to the structure of the one-particle Hilbert space.
Let ω be a centrally pure state. In appendix A.8 we show that, within the corresponding
Kµ space, the anti-self-adjoint operator R squares to −1 on the orthocomplement of its
kernel.
It follows that its spectrum is simply {+i, 0, −i}.

We dedicate the remainder of this subsection to showing the decomposition in equation
(6.12). To accomplish this, we consider a generic real test function f, and decompose it
within Kµ as
|f⟩µ = |fi + f−i⟩µ + |f0⟩µ.
(6.13)
Because R commutes with complex conjugation, each of the states in the above expression is
real, so one can consider the Weyl operators eiϕ[fi+f−i] and eiϕ[f0]. Because these correspond
to eigenstates of the R operators in distinct eigenspaces, the operators ϕ[fi +f−i] and ϕ[f0]
commute, so one has
eiϕ[f] = eiϕ[fi+f−i]eiϕ[f0].
(6.14)
Since Aω is the von Neumann algebra generated by all Weyl operators eiϕ[f], we learn
that we can decompose it as
Aω = Aω,±i ∨Aω,0,
(6.15)
with
Aω,±i = ⟨eiϕ[fi+f−i]⟩
(6.16)
and
Aω,0 = ⟨eiϕ[f0]⟩.
(6.17)
Since the commutator operator R annihilates |f0⟩µ, the algebra Aω,0 is clearly abelian.
It is clear that under the unitary equivalence in equation (6.11), the decomposition
from equation (6.15) becomes a tensor product. So all that remains is to show that under
this map, Aω,±i becomes the full algebra of bounded operators on Hω,i.26 To show this, all
we will need is that the commutant of Aω,±i acts trivially on Hω,i. This can be accomplished
using the commutator computations from appendix C.5. In the language of appendices C.2-
C.6, one associates, with any subset X of the one-particle Hilbert space, the von Neumann
algebra
A(X) = ⟨eiχα | |α⟩∈X⟩.
(6.18)
The χ operators, introduced above equation (5.9), obey the following property for any f±i
in the ±i eigenspaces of R:
χϕ[fi]|ω⟩= ϕ[fi + f−i].
(6.19)
26Recall from the comment below equation (6.12) that this automatically implies Aω,0 is the center of
Aω.

such that U|β⟩is orthogonal to the space R = +i. Since the image of U is automatically

tensor factor Hω,i within the decomposition Hω = Hω,i ⊗Hω,0. This completes the proof
of the claimed decomposition in equation (6.12).
6.2
Breaking excitability into tensor factors
Now suppose we have states ω1 and ω2, and assume that ω1 is centrally pure. For the
moment, we will not assume that ω2 is centrally pure. By the results of the preceding
subsection, one has a decomposition
Hω1 ∼= Hω1,i ⊗Hω1,0
(6.25)
with a corresponding decomposition of the von Neumann algebra
Aω1 ∼= B(Hω1,i) ⊗Zω1,0.
(6.26)
We wish to use this decomposition to simplify our understanding of the excitability relation
ω2 ≺ω1.
– 55 –


## Page 58


Our starting point is proposition 1.2 — see also remark 1.3 — which in the present
setting tells us that ω2 ≺ω1 is equivalent to the condition that the “identity map” on Weyl
operators extend to a normal homomorphism from Aω1 to Aω2. I.e., if one considers the map
α that takes eiϕ[f] acting on Hω1 to eiϕ[f] acting on Hω2, it is necessary and sufficient that
this map be a ∗-homomorphism that is continuous with respect to the ultraweak topology.
Suppose first that one has ω2 ≺ω1, so we know that α is a normal homomorphism.
We also know, from section 3.1, that ω2 ≺ω1 implies µ2 ≺µ1. Consequently, the map
L|f⟩µ1 = |f⟩µ2 is bounded. It follows that the map α, defined on test functions by
α(eiϕ[f]) = eiϕ[f],
(6.27)
uniquely extends by ultraweak continuity to generic real elements |ψ⟩µ1 in Kµ1 as
α(eiϕ[ψ]) = eiϕ[Lψ].
(6.28)
In particular, the algebra Aω1,±i from the preceding section is generated by operators eiϕ[ψ]
where |ψ⟩µ is in the support of R, while the algebra Aω1,0 is generated by operators eiϕ[ψ]
where |ψ⟩µ is in the kernel of R. Consequently, α has two restrictions:
α±i : Aω1,±i →Aω2,
(6.29)
α0 : Aω1,0 →Aω2,
(6.30)
each of which is a normal homomorphism, and each of which can be written explicitly in
terms of equation (6.28).
Going in the other direction, we now claim that in order to prove that α extends
to a normal homomorphism, it is sufficient to check that the maps α±i and α0 — which
initially are defined only on Weyl operators — extend to normal homomorphisms within
the appropriate subalgebras.27 This will give us a way to break the excitability condition
ω2 ≺ω1 into two pieces, one corresponding to Aω1,±i and one corresponding to Aω1,0.
To prove this claim, we start from the necessary condition µ2 ≺µ1, so that we can
make sense of the bounded “identity map” L : Kµ1 →Kµ2. Then equation (6.28) gives maps
α±i and α0 that are defined on operators of the form eiϕ[ψ] within Aω1,±i and Aω1,0. If we
suppose further that each of these extends to a normal homomorphism, then we get maps
as in equations (6.29) and (6.30), and after rewriting Hω1 ∼= Hω1,i ⊗Hω1,0, we can rewrite
these as maps
α±i : B(Hω1,i) →Aω2,
(6.31)
α0 : Zω1,0 →Aω2.
(6.32)
The map α±i is actually quite constrained thanks to the observation that B(Hω1,i) is a factor
algebra. It is a basic fact about von Neumann algebras that any normal homomorphism
27This fact, and many pieces of the argument below, were explained to us by Lauritz van Luijk.
– 56 –


## Page 59


out of a factor must be an isomorphism onto its image.28 Consequently, the image
α±i(B(Hω1,i)) ⊆Aω2
(6.33)
is an isomorphic copy of B(Hω1,i). This is not only a factor, it is a type I factor. As explained
e.g. in [23, section 7.1], this means that there is a tensor product decomposition of Hω2 as
Hω2 ∼= K ⊗F
(6.34)
such that we have
α±i(B(Hω1,i)) ∼= B(K) ⊗1F.
(6.35)
The image of α0, i.e. the algebra
α0(Zω1,0) ⊆Aω2,
(6.36)
necessarily commutes with the image of B(Hω1,i), thanks to the homomorphism property
of α. So with respect to the above decomposition, α0(Zω1,0) commutes with B(K), and
therefore acts entirely on the tensor factor F within Hω2. This implies that after writing Hω1
as Hω1,i ⊗Hω1,0, and after writing Hω2 as K ⊗F, we have the decomposition α = α±i ⊗α0.
A tensor product of normal homomorphisms is obviously a normal homomorphism; this
completes the proof of the claim.
How will this discussion help us prove the excitability criteria for centrally pure states
as in theorem 1.30? So far we have only assumed that ω1 is centrally pure; now we also
assume ω2 is centrally pure, so we have decompositions
Hω2 ∼= Hω2,i ⊗Hω2,0
(6.37)
and
Aω2 ∼= B(Hω2,i) ⊗Zω2,0.
(6.38)
A priori, the image of the restriction α0 was just some complicated subalgebra of
Aω2. But in fact, the image of α0 must lie within Zω2,0. This is because α0 is defined to
send elements eiϕ[ψ] with R1|ψ⟩µ = 0 to elements eiϕ[Lψ] acting on Hω2. But the identity
L†R2L = R1 tells us that L maps the kernel of R1 to the kernel of R2, which means
that α maps Zω1,0 to a subalgebra of Zω2,0. Hence, crucially, we can convert the task of
understanding whether α0 extends to a normal homomorphism, to the task of establishing
excitability conditions for Gaussian states on abelian algebras as in theorem 1.29. This will
be the task of the following subsection.
We remark that no such simple classification is possible for the image of α±i — this
will generally map B(Hω1,i) to a subalgebra that is entangled between the two factors on
the right-hand side of (6.38). But the restriction of ω1 to B(Hω1,i) is pure, so it is possible

the kernel of α±i is either everything — impossible, since we have α±i(1) = 1 — or nothing, i.e., α±i is
injective.

6.3
The abelian case
Now we turn to the excitability question for a free field algebra where the commutator van-
ishes, i.e., where we have Ω= 0. We have some basic field operators ϕ[f] that all mutually
commute with one another forming an algebra A0, and a pair of zero-mean Gaussian states
τ1 and τ2, where we are using the symbols τj for these abelian states so as not to confuse
them with the states ωj from the preceding subsection.
Our goal is to prove theorem 1.29, which says that we have τ2 ≺τ1 if and only if we
have (i) µ2 ≺µ1, (ii) ker Q = 0, and (iii) Q −1 Hilbert-Schmidt. Recall that the necessity
of conditions (i) and (ii) was already established in Section 3.
First we note that since A0 is abelian, the state |τj⟩is automatically cyclic and sep-
arating for Aτj within Hτj.29 Consequently, whenever we have τ2 ≺τ1, we can conclude
as in section 2.1 that by [24, corollary 5.24], there is in fact a vector representative for τ2
within Hτ1. As in the pure-state case of section 4, we will find that well-definedness of the
vector representative implies that Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt.
Unlike in the pure-state case, there will be a great deal of freedom in choosing our
vector representative, for we could always act on it with any unitary in Aτ1 and get an
equally good representative. There is, however, a canonical representative furnished by
Araki’s theory of the natural cone [31]. Theorem 7.1 of that paper implies the existence of
a preferred representative |τ ♮
2⟩. This vector has the special property that it is in the set

6.4
The general theorem
Now we put together theorems 1.27 and 1.29 — which deal with the mixed-from-pure case
and the abelian case, respectively — to prove the centrally pure excitability result, i.e.
theorem 1.30. As explained at the beginning of the section, this result (together with the
simple logic of section 5.4) automatically implies the general excitability theorem, theorem
1.31, which is the main result of the paper.
To prove theorem 1.30, we make use of the decomposition from section 6.2, which tells
us that for centrally pure states, we have ω2 ≺ω1 if and only if the following “restriction
maps” are ultraweakly continuous: the map α±i : Aω1,±i →Aω2 with
α±i(eiϕ[ψ]) = eiϕ[Lψ]

“abelian piece” of ω2.

7
Gaussian excitability implies quasiequivalence
In the previous sections, we proved the main theorem of the paper, theorem 1.31. This
theorem says that for zero-mean Gaussian states ω1 and ω2, the excitability relation ω2 ≺ω1
is equivalent to
(i) µ2 ≺µ1,
(ii) √X2 −√X1 is Hilbert-Schmidt, and
(iii) Q has trivial kernel.
In this section, we use this result to prove theorem 1.32, which states that whenever we
have ω2 ≺ω1 for zero-mean Gaussian states, we automatically have ω1 ≺ω2. This should
be contrasted with the discussion of one-way excitability in section 2.2. There, it was shown
that for any non-factorial state ω1, there exists a state ω2 with ω2 ≺ω1 and ω1 ̸≺ω2. The
key point of the present section is that this situation cannot occur when both states are
zero-mean Gaussian states.
The main observation is that condition (ii) above, which states that √X2 −√X1 is
Hilbert-Schmidt, necessarily implies that Q−1 is Hilbert-Schmidt as well, since Q−1 = X2−
X1 can be obtained by left- and right-multiplying the Hilbert-Schmidt operator √X2−√X1
by the bounded operator √X2 + √X1. So whenever we have ω2 ≺ω1, we necessarily have
43See again footnote 41, with O ≡Y −iR1 and M ≡Y −1/2.
– 70 –


## Page 73


that Q −1 is Hilbert-Schmidt. This means that Q must be invertible away from its kernel,
since it cannot have infinitely many eigenvalues accumulating near zero. But since ω2 ≺ω1
implies condition (iii) above, which is that Q has trivial kernel, we learn that Q is always
invertible when we have ω2 ≺ω1.
Invertibility of Q is the statement that µ1 and µ2 induce equivalent inner products on
the space of smearing functions. So we automatically have µ1 ≺µ2, and if we define an
operator Q′ on Kµ2 that reproduces the µ1 inner product, we know that Q′ has no kernel.
Consequently, the versions of conditions (i) and (iii) for the relation ω1 ≺ω2 are satisfied.

– 71 –


## Page 74


8
Discussion
The main point of this paper was to give a careful treatment of the “excitability question,”
which is a physical cousin of the mathematical “quasiequivalence question” that has been
discussed by algebraic quantum field theorists. We established general algebraic criteria
for excitability, and explained how it is possible in a general theory to have an excitability
relation ω2 ≺ω1 without having the converse ω1 ≺ω2. In a lengthy proof that involved
several nested layers of argument, we then established theorem 1.31, which gives general
criteria for excitability in terms of a “finite-trace condition” when both ω1 and ω2 are
zero-mean Gaussian states in a free theory. We also established several other theorems —
theorems 1.18, 1.27, and 1.30 — in which we provided alternate, simplified criteria that one
can check in special cases where ω1 or ω2 has additional structure.
A surprising-to-us consequence of our analysis is that for zero-mean Gaussian states,
one-way excitability always implies quasiequivalence.
This did not have to be true —
indeed, in section 2, we gave examples of algebraic states in which one-way excitability is
strict. The additional assumption that the excited state is Gaussian, however, kills these
examples.
A few immediate applications of our analysis spring to mind. We summarize them
below.
(i) There are settings in which one would like to construct, within a given sector, a set
of correlation functions that have useful features. One example is provided by the
geometric modular flow conjecture from [6]. The analysis of the present paper allows
this question to be answered, at least in principle, in free field theory. A forthcoming
paper by one of us with Gautam Satishchandran will explore this point further [36].
(ii) Related to the above, since our analysis works for any free field theory — including
generalized free fields! — it can be used to study sector equivalence within the large-N
limit of AdS/CFT, which has many interesting implications as discussed in e.g. [9–11].
It may also be possible to apply these ideas to the semiclassical limit of closed-universe
quantum gravity as formulated in [37].
(iii) Strict one-way excitability is intimately related to the existence of a center for the von
Neumann algebra associated with an algebraic state. In Klein-Gordon theory, one
knows thanks to [5] that such a situation does not arise in physical (i.e., “Hadamard”)
states. Even if it did, our analysis would show that one-way excitability cannot happen
for Gaussian states.
However, there are important field theories in which a center for the von Neumann
algebra is unavoidable: these are theories with higher-form symmetries [38], where
topological operators of codimension greater than one will necessarily appear in the
center of any local algebra. It would be interesting to study whether one-way ex-
citability in such a setting reveals anything about the structure of the symmetry.
(iv) Much of the actual technical material of this paper was concerned with nitty-gritty
details of free, Gaussian states. The simplicity of this theory allowed us to argue at
– 72 –


## Page 75


a high level of rigor; on the other hand, even in this simple setting, the arguments
are quite complicated! It would be very interesting to understand if the algebraic
approach to excitability lets one make interesting statements about when excitability
is possible in interacting theories, even if one must sacrifice rigor in the process.

and Engineering Research Council of Canada through Vanier Canada Graduate Scholarships
[Funding Reference Number: CGV–192707]. FC acknowledges support from a Graduate

A
(Generalized) free field theory
This appendix provides an overview of the algebraic theory of free quantum fields. The
framework presented here encompasses both Klein-Gordon theory and generalized free field
theory. Our presentation differs somewhat from the classic literature, and some of our proof
techniques are new. The earlier reviews from which we extrapolated much of this material
are [39] and [28].

A.2
Basics
From the algebraic perspective, a (generalized) free field theory is a collection of fields on
spacetime that satisfy a “c-number” commutation relation. One starts with a spacetime M
and considers the collection C∞
0 (M) of “test functions” — smooth, compactly supported
functions from M to C. To each test function f, one associates an object ϕ[f], which is
supposed to represent the formal expression
ϕ[f] ≡
Z
dDx
p
|g|ϕ(x)f(x),
(A.20)
with ϕ(x) the generalized free field.
The fact that the field theory is “free” is encoded in the statement that the commutator
[ϕ(x), ϕ(y)] is proportional to the identity. Formally, one writes
[ϕ(x), ϕ(y)] = −iΩ(x, y),
(A.21)
with Ωan antisymmetric distribution on two copies of M. The choice of Ωis part of the
definition of the theory.
By smearing, one translates equation (A.21) into an algebraic
equation
[ϕ[f], ϕ[g]] = −iΩ[f, g].
(A.22)
The abstract ∗-algebra of the generalized free field theory is the collection of polynomials
made out of smeared fields ϕ[f], subject to the commutation relation (A.22), also subject to
a linearity condition ϕ[αf +g] = αϕ[f]+ϕ[g], and finally endowed with an adjoint operation
that corresponds to complex conjugation:
ϕ[f]∗≡ϕ[f∗].
(A.23)
This ∗-algebra is denoted A0, or sometimes A0(M).
For a generalized free field theory, the data we have discussed so far is all that goes
into the definition of the theory. In Klein-Gordon theory, one has additional data supplied
by the equation of motion. If M is a globally hyperbolic spacetime, then the Klein-Gordon
equation (□−m2)ϕ = 0 admits fundamental “advanced” and “retarded” propagators [40],
which are kernels A and R such that one has
(□−m2)(Af) = f
(A.24)
with Af vanishing to the future of the support of f, and similarly for Rf but with Rf
vanishing to the past of the support of f. The “advanced-minus-retarded” kernel E = A−R
maps test functions to solutions:
(□−m2)(Ef) = 0.
(A.25)
The first way that the equations of motion show up in algebraic Klein-Gordon theory is
through the canonical commutation relation. In particular, the commutator from equation
(A.22) is determined by setting
Ω(x, y) = E(x, y),
(A.26)
– 76 –


## Page 79


which comes from canonical quantization of the symplectic form on the Klein-Gordon phase
space.
By abuse of notation we will often refer to Ωas the “symplectic form” even in
the context of generalized free field theory, where Ωis actually just an antisymmetric
distribution on two copies of M.
Another important way in which the equations of motion show up is that in algebraic
Klein-Gordon theory, one imposes (□−m2)ϕ = 0 on the abstract free field via integration
by parts. This amounts to subjecting the ∗-algebra A0 to the algebraic equivalence relation
ϕ[(□−m2)f] = 0,
(A.27)
or, equivalently, quotienting the space of test functions by im(□−m2). Effectively, one is
smearing the free field ϕ(x) against elements of the space
S ≡C∞
0 (M)/im(□−m2).
(A.28)
The choice of symbol “S ” is deliberate; one can actually show that S is isomorphic
to the phase space of solutions of Klein-Gordon theory that are smooth and that have
compact support on any spatial slice. The isomorphism is simply the map [f] 7→Ef. That
this map is well defined follows from the compatibility of E with the equations of motion,
i.e., E(□−m2)f = 0. That the map is an isomorphism follows from the facts — see [39,
lemma 3.2.1] — that f 7→Ef is surjective onto phase space, and that the kernel of this
map is exactly the image of the equations of motion.
A.3
Gaussian states and normal ordering
Given an abstract ∗-algebra A0, like the ∗-algebra of free fields discussed in the previous
subsection, one constructs Hilbert space representations via the GNS construction. To do
this, one specifies a linear functional ω : A0 →C that satisfies the positivity condition
ω(a∗a) ≥0.
(A.29)
This functional is supposed to represent the set of correlation functions for an abstract
quantum state. The GNS construction allows one to realize these correlation functions on
a Hilbert space. To do this, one starts with the abstract vector space
Vω = {|aω⟩| a ∈A0},
(A.30)
and endows this space with the inner product
⟨aω|bω⟩= ω(a∗b).
(A.31)
Taking the quotient by the space of null states, then taking a Hilbert space completion, one
obtains a Hilbert space Hω whose elements are (Cauchy sequences of) equivalence classes
[|aω⟩]. There is rarely any confusion in simply writing |aω⟩for the corresponding equivalence
class. The Hilbert space Hω carries an action of the ∗-algebra A0 via the formula
b|aω⟩= |(ba)ω⟩.
(A.32)
– 77 –


## Page 80


This tells us how each element b of the ∗-algebra should act on states of the form a|ω⟩
with a ∈A0. However, this action will generally be unbounded, and cannot be completed
continuously to an action on all of Hω. We say that the operators in A0 are represented on
Hω as unbounded operators with a common dense domain {a|ω⟩}. For more on unbounded
operators, especially as they appear in the GNS construction, see [21, appendix A].
Given a (generalized) free field algebra, there are some nice simplifications due to the
fact that the canonical commutation relation is proportional to the identity. This algebraic
feature means that free field algebras support Gaussian states, where all n-point functions
are determined from the 1- and 2-point functions. They are defined by starting with the
formal expression for connected n-point functions,

The notation here may seem a little confusing. Why did we call the creation operator

There is another subtlety we must address, which is that verifying equation (A.54)
is not actually enough to show that a[g]† is genuinely the adjoint of a[g]. The reason for
this is that the operators are unbounded. Equations (A.52) and (A.53) define the creation
and annihilation operators on Vω, which is a dense subspace of Hω. But these operators
are not bounded, so they cannot be extended to all of Hω. What equation (A.54) tells us
is that the genuine adjoint of a[g] is an extension of the creation operator “a[g]†” — i.e.,
these operators agree on all vectors where both are defined. Usefully, this tells us that
the adjoint of a[g] is densely defined (since the creation operator is densely defined), and
consequently a[g] is a “closable operator” — see the review in [25, section 2.3] for details.
A similar argument tells us that the creation operator is a closable operator. By abuse
of notation, we can use the symbols a[g] and a[g]† to denote the closures of the operators
defined by equations (A.52) and (A.53). Under this convention, it can be shown that these
operators are genuinely adjoints of one another, though the proof of this statement is a
little outside our present scope, so we present it in appendix B. An interesting consequence
of this statement is that the field operator eϕ[f], upon taking closures, has adjoint eϕ[f∗].
Consequently, the field operator eϕ[f] is self-adjoint for real f, which means it is a genuine
physical observable.

A.5
Quantizing phase space
The “Fock structure” described above tells us that when studying a Gaussian state of a
free field theory, all of the data of the GNS representation is really contained within the
one-particle Hilbert space H(1)
ω .45 There is another way of arriving at this Hilbert space
that is very useful for certain proofs. This is to obtain H(1)
ω
as a subspace of an enlarged
Hilbert space that is obtained by “quantizing the phase space of the theory,” in other words
by promoting the phase space to a Hilbert space.
One begins with the space of complex smearing functions C∞
0 (M). This generates the
∗-algebra A0, on which one defines a Gaussian state ω.
The GNS space Hω is obtained by using ω to induce an inner product on A0, then
taking the quotient by null states and performing a Hilbert space completion.
A different Hilbert space is produced by symmetrizing ω to obtain an inner product
directly on C∞
0 (M). This inner product, written µ, is defined by

By the “phase space” of the theory, we will mean the vector space Sµ obtained by

0 (M) with the µ inner product, then taking the quotient by null states:

0 (M)/ ker µ .

The reason we call this a “phase space” is that in the case of Klein-Gordon theory, one has
ker µ = ker Ω— as we will now demonstrate — which gives Sµ = S , the usual Klein-
Gordon phase space introduced in equation (A.28). Note that in any free theory, for u, v
real, one can easily show
⟨u + iv|u + iv⟩µ = ⟨u|u⟩µ + ⟨v|v⟩µ,
(A.64)
which implies that if u + iv is null for µ, then its real and imaginary parts are null as
well. Inequality (A.62) then implies that u and v, hence u + iv, are degeneracies of the
symplectic form. This gives the general inclusion ker µ ⊆ker Ω, and our task is to show
that in Klein-Gordon theory, the converse inclusion also holds.
In the case of Klein-Gordon theory, the symplectic form is Ω= E, with E the advanced-
minus-retarded propagator. As discussed above, the only degeneracies of Ωconsist of test
functions that are in the image of the equations of motion, i.e., we have ker Ω= im(□−m2).
But in Klein-Gordon theory, the ∗-algebra A0 involves a quotient by the images of the
equations of motion, so everything in the image of the equations of motion is a degeneracy
not just of Ω, but also of any algebraic state ω (see footnote 44). By equation (A.59), this
implies that every element of ker Ωis itself a degeneracy of the inner product µ.
Note that in generalized free field theories, the quotient by kerµ might not kill all of
the degeneracies of the symplectic form. In this case, the space Sµ is µ-dependent.
Now that we know µ is a positive definite inner product on the phase space Sµ, we
can define its Hilbert space completion Kµ. The utility of having constructed Kµ is that it
retains some information pertaining to the state ω that is discarded in the construction of
Hω. In particular, if there exist test functions f with46
ω(eϕ[f∗]eϕ[f]) = 0
(A.65)
but for which f is not a degeneracy of µ, then there will be a state in Kµ representing f,
while no such state is present in Hω. In this sense, null states for ω can be kept alive within
the space Kµ.
46Note this cannot happen if the state ω is faithful, but we will see later that many states of interest in
free field theory are not faithful; in particular, no pure state is faithful.
