A.6
von Neumann algebras generated by the fields
Given a ∗-algebra A0 and a GNS representation Hω, there are a few ways to “complete”
A0 into a von Neumann algebra Aω acting on Hω. One natural way is to declare that Aω
is the smallest von Neumann algebra such that all of the operators coming from A0 are
“affiliated” to A0. For more details on affiliation and this construction, see [21, appendix A]
and the discussion below. For other choices, see [41, 42].
Usefully, when A0 is a free field algebra and ω is a Gaussian state, there is really
only one canonical choice of von Neumann algebra in the GNS representation Hω, and it
corresponds to the choice Aω described in the preceding paragraph. The main claim of this
subsection is in the free field setting, Aω can be described in any of three equivalent ways:
• the smallest von Neumann algebra such that each ϕ[f] is affiliated, called Aω,
• the smallest von Neumann algebra containing all bounded functions of ϕ[f] with f
real, called Aself-adjoint,
• the smallest von Neumann algebra containing all Weyl operators eiϕ[f] with f real.
Note that for the second and third bullet points to make sense, ϕ[f] must have self-adjoint
closure when f is real, but this is exactly what we showed in appendix B. We also comment
that for the purposes of this discussion, the distinction between ϕ[f] and eϕ[f] is immaterial
— they generate the same von Neumann algebras, since they only differ by a multiple of
the identity.
– 85 –


## Page 88


different choices one might make for Aω all give rise to the same algebra. The main
reason for this is that, as discussed in appendix B, the free field operator ϕ[f] has self-adjoint
closure whenever f is real. For any self-adjoint operator T, one can act on T with bounded
functions to produce bounded operators. A natural candidate for Aω is the smallest von
Neumann algebra containing all bounded functions of ϕ[f] for f real. One might worry
that this is smaller than the algebra discussed above, since it seems to only know about the
self-adjoint elements of A0, but we will see in this section that the two definitions coincide.
We will also see that it is equivalent to the smallest von Neumann algebra containing all
of the “Weyl operators” eiϕ[f], where f is real. For the purposes of this discussion, the
distinction between ϕ[f] and eϕ[f] is immaterial — they generate the same von Neumann
algebras, since they only differ by a multiple of the identity.
We begin by relating the algebras in the first two bullet points.
For a general ∗-algebra A0 and state ω, one can consider as in [21, appendix A.4] the
polar decomposition of any a ∈A0 as it acts on Hω:
a = Va|a|.
(A.73)
The minimal affiliated algebra Aω is the smallest von Neumann algebra containing all partial
isometries Va and all bounded functions of the positive operators |a|. For the self-adjoint
operator ϕ[f], the polar decomposition is of the form
ϕ[f] = Vf|ϕ[f]|,
(A.74)
where Vf is simply equal to +1 on the portion of Hilbert space where ϕ[f] has positive
spectrum, equal to −1 on the portion of Hilbert space where ϕ[f] has negative spectrum,
and equal to 0 on the portion of Hilbert space where ϕ[f] is zero. The operator Vf is self-
adjoint, so the von Neumann algebra it generates includes the projectors onto the +1, 0,
and −1 eigenspaces of Vf,48 which are the projections onto the positive and negative parts
of the spectrum of ϕ[f]. By combining these projectors with bounded functions of |ϕ[f]|,
one can obtain any bounded function of ϕ[f].49 This guarantees that the von Neumann
algebra generated by the self-adjoint elements ϕ[f] is contained in the minimal affiliated
algebra Aω:
Aself-adjoint ⊆Aω.
(A.76)

In [21, appendix A], it was shown that the commutant of Aω is the set of all bounded
operators that commute with all operators in A0 and all adjoints of operators in A0.50
When one talks about a bounded operator T commuting with an unbounded operator a,
one means that T preserves the domain of a, and that one has Ta = aT on that domain. So
we are now aiming to show that if a bounded operator commutes with all of the self-adjoint
operators ϕ[f] with f real, then it commutes with all operators in A0 and their adjoints.
To show that a bounded operator T commutes with an unbounded operator a, it suffices
to show commutativity on a “core” for a. A core for an unbounded operator is a subspace
of the domain such that the full action of a can be determined by taking limits from this
subspace. By construction, all of the operators coming from A0 are initially defined on the
GNS domain Vω, then “closed” by taking limits. So all of these operators have Vω as a core.
Now, suppose that T formally commutes with all ϕ[f] for f real. Consider a product
ϕ[f1] . . . ϕ[fn], with each fj real. Suppose that |ψ⟩is in the GNS domain Vω. Then because
T formally commutes with ϕ[fn], we know that T|ψ⟩is in the domain of ϕ[fn], and satisfies
ϕ[fn]T|ψ⟩= Tϕ[fn]|ψ⟩.
(A.78)
But ϕ[fn]|ψ⟩is in the domain of ϕ[fn−1]; since T formally commutes with ϕ[fn−1], the
vector Tϕ[fn]|ψ⟩is in the domain of ϕ[fn−1], and we have
ϕ[fn−1]ϕ[fn]T|ψ⟩= Tϕ[fn−1]ϕ[fn]|ψ⟩.
(A.79)
Clearly we can proceed by induction to learn that T|ψ⟩is in the domain of ϕ[f1] . . . ϕ[fn]
and that T commutes with this field monomial on the GNS domain. The same obviously
holds for any linear combination of field monomials, and since the field is linear (ϕ[f +ig] =
ϕ[f] + iϕ[g]) an arbitrary field polynomial can be written as a linear combination of field
monomials with purely real smearing functions. It follows that in the case of the free field
algebra, we have A′
self-adjoint ⊆A′
ω, and the von Neumann algebra generated by the self-
adjoint operators ϕ[f] with f real is exactly the same as the minimal von Neumann algebra
for which the full ∗-algebra A0 is affiliated.
Now we turn our attention to the Weyl operators. For f real, the operator ϕ[f] is
self-adjoint, so one can consider its exponential eiϕ[f]. This is called a Weyl operator for the
free field. They can be useful to consider for certain calculations as the Weyl operators are
bounded operators, while the field operators ϕ[f] are unbounded. In fact, some approaches
to free field theory take the Weyl operators as the core objects of the theory, and start with
an abstract C∗algebra of free fields.
The set of Weyl operators eiϕ[f] generates the exact same von Neumann algebra as
the set of self-adjoint field operators ϕ[f]. Since eiϕ[f] is a bounded function of ϕ[f], it is
obviously contained in the von Neumann algebra generated by ϕ[f]. Conversely, by the
spectral theorem, one has for any vectors |ψ⟩and |χ⟩a measure νψ,η on the real line such

already showed that ϕ[f]† is the closure of ϕ[f ∗].

Now that we have constructed these generalized Weyl operators, we can use them, as
suggested by our notation so far, to define operators eϕ[ψ] acting on Hω for any |ψ⟩µ in the
completed phase space Kµ, not just for test functions. This is because the arguments above
guarantee that the map t →eieϕ[tψ] is a strongly continuous one-parameter unitary group
for any real |ψ⟩µ in Kµ. By Stone’s theorem, such a group admits a unique self-adjoint
generator, which we define to be eϕ[ψ]. Field operators ϕ[ψ] for non-real ψ can be obtained
by using this procedure to obtain the real and imaginary parts independently.

More on pure states
A state ω on a ∗-algebra A0 is said to be pure if it cannot be decomposed as a nontrivial
convex combination of other states,
ω = pω1 + (1 −p)ω2.
(A.108)
In the case of a free field algebra, an equivalent condition — see for example52 [21, appendix
A.7] — is that the von Neumann algebra generated by A0 on Hω has trivial commutant.

Another interesting class of states are those that are “centrally pure.” This means that
while the von Neumann algebra Aω generated by A0 has a commutant, the commutant is
contained entirely within Aω, i.e., the commutant is the center. In this case, by its defining
equation (A.66), R will have a kernel, but we will now show that we have R2 = −1 on the
orthocomplement of the kernel.
We note that for real u, the condition R2|u⟩µ = −|u⟩µ is equivalent to the statement
that ϕ[u + iRu] annihilates |ω⟩in the GNS Hilbert space, for this occurs if and only if
|u + iRu⟩µ lies in the (−i) eigenspace of R, and for real |u⟩µ this is equivalent to R2 |u⟩µ =
−|u⟩µ. So we consider a real state |u⟩µ in the orthocomplement of the kernel of R, and we
define
|ψ⟩= ϕ[u + iRu]|ω⟩,

B
Adjoints of ladder operators
This appendix fills in the details of a claim made in appendix A.4. Namely, given a Gaussian
state ω, we defined the creation and annihilation operators
eϕ[f] = a[f] + a[f∗]†,
(B.1)
and claimed that upon taking closures, a[f]† is genuinely the adjoint of a[f].55
To prove this, we recall the definition of the adjoint of an unbounded operator — again,
see [25, section 2.3] for a review. If T is an unbounded operator defined on a dense domain
D ⊆H, then the adjoint T † is defined on all vectors |ψ⟩such that the functional
|χ⟩7→⟨ψ|Tχ⟩
(B.2)

(B.3)
and density of D tells us that T †|ψ⟩is unique. This defines the action of T † on its domain.
Now, in appendix A.4, we already explained that the adjoint of a[f] is an extension of

C
One-particle modular theory
The Tomita operator S = J∆1/2 associated with a Gaussian state has an analogue, s =
jδ1/2, acting only on the one-particle space H(1)
ω . These operators are best understood by
writing the n-particle Fock spaces explicitly as n-th symmetric tensor powers of H(1)
ω
via
the identification of equation (A.56):
: ϕ[f1] . . . ϕ[fn] : |ω⟩↔
√
n!|ϕ[f1]ω⟩⊗S · · · ⊗S |ϕ[fn]ω⟩.
(C.1)
56This just comes from the definition of a closed operator — the domain of the closure of a[f]† is the
set of vectors that can be reached by limits of sequences in Vω such that the “image sequence” obtained by
acting with a[f]† also converges.
– 97 –


## Page 100


On the GNS domain, the connection between S and s is via the formula
S (|ϕ[f1]ω⟩⊗S · · · ⊗S |ϕ[fn]ω⟩) = |sϕ[f1]ω⟩⊗S · · · ⊗S |sϕ[fn]ω⟩,
(C.2)
and similarly for the connection between J and j, and the connection between ∆1/2 and
δ1/2. By mapping modular operators from the full GNS space Hω to the one-particle space
H(1)
ω , one obtains many simplifications that can be used, for example, to prove a general
form of Haag duality.
This appendix elaborates that theory and its main results.
We
mostly follow [45] and [44, section 6.5], though note that the Weyl operators there are not
equivalent to our operators eiχψ below.57
To simplify the presentation in this appendix, we drop the “tilde” notation eϕ that was
used in the previous two appendices; we will write ϕ for the vacuum-subtracted field. For
zero-mean Gaussian states, like those considered in the main text, there is no difference
between these two conventions.
C.1
Key points
• Let ω be a Gaussian state with one-particle Hilbert space H(1)

C.3
Cyclic and separating
We are now ready to address an important question for the construction of modular theory:
under what conditions on a closed, real subspace X can we conclude that |ω⟩is cyclic or
separating for the von Neumann algebra A(X)?
First we show that cyclicity is equivalent to the statement that the complex-linear span
of X is dense in H(1)
ω . By taking derivatives of operators like eiχψ, one can obtain, within
the set A(X)|ω⟩, any vector of the form
χψ1 . . . χψn|ω⟩
|ψj⟩∈X,
(C.21)
and such vectors are dense within A(X)|ω⟩. By normal-ordering the χ operators with an
analog of equation (A.39), one sees that, under the unitary equivalence (A.56), the set of
all linear combinations of such states is exactly the set of all linear combinations of states
in (X + iX) ⊗S · · · ⊗S (X + iX). The closure of this set is equal to the full symmetric Fock
space if and only if X + iX is dense in H(1)
ω . Since the symmetric Fock space of H(1)
ω
is
canonically isomorphic to Hω through equation (A.56), the claim is proved.
Now we show that |ω⟩is separating for A(X) if and only if one has X ∩iX = {0}.59
For the easy direction, we assume the existence of a nonzero vector |ψ⟩in X ∩iX. Note
59Recall we have assumed (without loss of generality) that X is a real vector space, so the zero vector is
always contained in both X and iX.

The spaces X and X′ have nice

(ψ, η) = Re⟨ψ|η⟩,

upon recognizing that X ∩iX and X′ + iX′ are both complex Hilbert spaces, and they
are easily seen to be one another’s orthocomplements with respect to the original, complex
inner product.
So far, we have shown that whenever we have X ∩iX = {0}, we have X′ + iX′ dense,
so we may conclude that |ω⟩is cyclic for A(X′). Given the inclusion A(X′) ⊆A(X)′,
clearly any cyclic vector for A(X′) is also cyclic for A(X)′. This tells us that when we have
X ∩iX = {0}, the vector |ω⟩is cyclic for A(X)′, hence separating for A(X).
Another useful statement is that for two closed, real subspaces X, Y ⊆H(1)
ω , one has
A(X) = A(Y ) if and only if one has X = Y. The “if” direction is obvious. For the “only if”
direction, one first observes the identity
A(X + Y ) = A(X) ∨A(Y ),

We now apply the modular theory toolkit that we have developed so far to discuss Haag
duality. In appendix C.3, we proved
A(X′) ⊆A(X)′.
(C.54)
We now prove equality. Without loss of generality we take X to be closed and real-linear.
In the case where |ω⟩is cyclic and separating for X, the proof is very simple. Using
equation (C.47), one may easily compute

A(X)′ = JA(X)J = A(jX).

So all that remains is to show the inclusion jX ⊆X′. To see this, fix |ψ⟩, |η⟩∈X, and

if |ω⟩is cyclic and separating for A(X).

Putting all this together, we find that A(X)′ is minimally generated by (i) the set on

Y = (X + iX)⊥+ {|ψ⟩∈C | Im⟨ψ|η⟩= 0 for all |η⟩∈C ∩X}.

One consequence of Haag duality that is used in appendix A.8 is the dual of the identity
A(X + Y ) = A(X)∨A(Y ) from equation (C.27), which we proved in appendix C.3. Taking
commutants and using Haag duality, one discovers
A((X + Y )′) = A(X′) ∩A(Y ′),
(C.73)
and it is easy to compute (X + Y )′ = X′ ∩Y ′, giving
A(X′ ∩Y ′) = A(X′) ∩A(Y ′).
(C.74)
Relabeling X′ →X and Y ′ →Y , it is easy to see that we have A(X ∩Y ) = A(X) ∩A(Y )
for general X, Y.
C.6
Non-cyclic-and-separating
We now return to our discussion of the one-particle modular operators (s, j, δ), generalizing
the results of section C.4 to the non-cyclic-and-separating case. Our main reason for devel-
oping this theory is that — as discussed in appendix C.4 — any Gaussian state for which
the spectrum of R contains eigenvalues ±i will not be separating for the von Neumann
algebra generated by fields. In particular, this is the case for all pure states, so it is useful
to understand how to apply modular theory in this setting.
In ordinary modular theory, when one has a state |ω⟩and a von Neumann algebra A,
one defines the projector P onto the subspace A′|ω⟩— written P because it is an element
of A — and the projector P ′ onto the subspace A|ω⟩— written P ′ because it is an element
of A′. When one has P ̸= 1 the state is not separating; when one has P ′ ̸= 1 the state is
not cyclic. In this case one can still define modular operators — see for example the nice
review in [47, appendix A] — via the formulas68
Sa|ω⟩= Pa†|ω⟩,
S(1 −P ′) = 0
(C.75)
and
S′a′|ω⟩= P ′(a′)†|ω⟩,
S′(1 −P) = 0.
(C.76)
67Clearly elements of (X + iX)⊥have real overlap with X, since such overlaps vanish. Moreover, states
|ψ⟩in the second term have real overlaps with both C⊥∩X (since such overlaps vanish) and C ∩X (by
definition), and thus with all of (C⊥∩X) ∪(C ∩X) = X; it follows that such states are in X′.
68The inclusion of P in the definition of S is necessary to ensure that S is well defined in the non-
separating case. In particular, if we have a |ω⟩= 0 for some nonzero a ∈A, we must ensure the identity
Sa |ω⟩= 0 as well. Introducing P does the job, since it is easy to show that for such a, a† |ω⟩must be
orthogonal to A′ |ω⟩, thus Pa† |ω⟩= 0. The formula S(1 −P ′) = 0 simply amounts to defining S to vanish
on the orthocomplement of A |ω⟩, which is nontrivial in the non-cyclic case.
– 108 –


## Page 111


It is an easy exercise to show that (S′)† is an extension of S, and that S† is an extension of
S′, so that each operator is closable and can be replaced by its closure with no confusion.
Given a real, closed subspace X of the one-particle Hilbert space H(1)
ω , we can still
define X′ as the real-orthocomplement of (iX). We define p to be the projector onto the
closure of X′ + iX′, and define p′ to be the projector onto the closure of X + iX. By
comparison to the usual modular operators, it is natural to define operators s and s′ via
s|x + iy⟩= p|x −iy⟩,
s(1 −p′) = 0
(C.77)
and
s′|x′ + iy′⟩= p′|x −iy⟩,
s′(1 −p) = 0.
(C.78)
It is straightforward to check that (s′)† extends s, and that s† extends s′.
In this setting, for ψ1, . . . , ψn ∈X, we have69
Sχψ1 . . . χψn|ω⟩= Pχψn . . . χψ1|ω⟩,
(C.79)
and by using normal ordering and the isomorphism between the GNS space and a symmetric
Fock space, we can conclude that S acts on the real elements within each n-particle space
as
S|ψ1 ⊗S · · · ⊗S ψn⟩= P|ψ1 ⊗S · · · ⊗S ψn⟩.
(C.80)
Going forward, the important fact will be that P acts on each n-particle subspace as
p⊗n. The reason is that P is the projector onto the closure of the space A(X)′|ω⟩. But by
the preceding subsection, we know this is the same as the closure of the space A(X′)|ω⟩,
which is simply the sum over the n-particle subspaces (X′ + iX′)⊗n. From this, we may
conclude that for ψ1, . . . , ψn ∈X, we have
S|ψ1 ⊗S · · · ⊗S ψn⟩= |pψ1 ⊗S · · · ⊗S pψn⟩,
(C.81)
and extending by antilinearity, we find that so long as each ψj is in the domain of s, we
have
S|ψ1 ⊗S · · · ⊗S ψn⟩= |sψ1 ⊗S · · · ⊗S sψn⟩.
(C.82)
So the usual identity still holds; S acts on the n-particle spaces as a tensor product of s op-
erators, and similarly for the operators J, ∆and j, δ that appear in the polar decompositions
of S and s.

E.4
Domains of field operators
Here we aim to show that |ω2⟩, as defined by the ansatz of section 4, is in the domain of
(the closure of) each field operator ϕ[f]. Proceeding by induction, we will show that all
states of the form ϕ[f1] . . . ϕ[fn]|ω2⟩make sense.
The point is that, as explained in appendix B, each field operator ϕ[f] has, as its
closure, the adjoint of the field operator ϕ[f ∗]. So at first it will suffice to show that |ω2⟩
is in the domain of the adjoint of ϕ[f ∗]. By the definition of the adjoint of an unbounded

F
Some Schatten-class counterexamples
The general condition claimed in the main text for excitability ω2 ≺ω1 — see theorem
1.31 — is that one has µ2 ≺µ1, that the operator √X2 −√X1 is Hilbert-Schmidt, and
that Q = X2 −X1 + 1 has trivial kernel. When ω2 and ω1 are both pure, instead of the
condition on √X2 −√X1, one may equivalently check that X2 −X1 is Hilbert-Schmidt —
see proposition 1.21. When ω1 is pure and ω2 is mixed, one may equivalently check that
the “diagonal blocks” of X2 −X1 with respect to the R1 decomposition are trace class —
see theorem 1.27. In this appendix we show that these statements are tight:
• First we show that for any pure state ω1 there exists a state ω2 with X2 −X1 Hilbert-
Schmidt but √X2 −√X1 is not Hilbert-Schmidt.
• Second we show that for any pure state ω1 there exists a state ω2 ≺ω1 — so in
particular the diagonal blocks of X2−X1 are trace-class — for which the full operator
X2 −X1 is not trace-class.
F.1

## Page 127


G.4
Invertibility of Q
Above equation (6.58) in the main text, we claimed that Q is invertible whenever we have
the Gaussian-state excitability relation τ2 ≺τ1 on an abelian scalar field algebra. Notation
used in this appendix is the same as the notation used in section 6.3.
Invertibility of Q is the statement that µ1 and µ2 induce equivalent inner products on
the space of smearing functions. From τ2 ≺τ1, we already have µ2 ≺µ1. Hence, it remains
to show that µ1 ≺µ2. To do so, we will show that any sequence of test functions fn that
converges to zero with respect to µ2 must also converge to zero with respect to µ1. As in
section 3.1, it suffices to consider real sequences.
We begin by evaluating
⟨τ ♮

= e−∥g+fn+h∥2

(G.22)
This shows that if fn →0 in Kµ2, then eiϕ[fn] →1 with respect to matrix elements for a
dense set of states within the subspace of Hτ1 generated by the action of Weyl operators
on |τ ♮
2⟩. Since the sequence eiϕ[fn] is uniformly bounded, that means that the sequence
actually converges with respect to the weak topology for this subspace.
We now claim that this subspace coincides with the entire GNS Hilbert space Hτ1; that
is, we claim |τ ♮
2⟩is cyclic for Aτ1. This follows from the fact that |τ ♮
2⟩lies in the natural
cone for Aτ1 (by construction) and is separating for Aτ1 (since it is separating on each
subalgebra Aτ1,(m)),73 and any separating state in the natural cone is automatically cyclic
[31, theorem 5.4].
So, eiϕ[fn] converges weakly to 1 for the full space Hτ1. In particular, evaluating on the
GNS vector |τ1⟩gives
⟨τ1| eiϕ[fn] |τ1⟩= e−∥fn∥2
µ1/2 →1,
(G.23)
which implies that fn must converge to zero also with respect to µ1, which concludes the
proof.
